Mathematics · Version 8 · corrected after the second review · 5 October 2026

The Prop Firm Option

The prop-firm programmes worth trading under each firm's rules and the plan's compliance policies, optimised setting by setting and derived step by step: how much one person can expect per month, and the mathematics behind every number.

$62,198 expected per month in the first year, one person, recommended plan

Expected values under a model of fair prices, for a trader with no predictive skill, under each firm's written rules as read on 4 October 2026. Firms may refuse payouts or apply behavioural rules at their discretion (Chapters 29 and 31). Not investment advice. Do not share or sell this plan: several firms close traders who run the same marketed strategy together.

The answer

Expected cash per month, one person

$62,198 a month in the first year, ± $2,116 (95%)

That is the expected cash, after the fees modelled (evaluation fees, activation and monthly fees, refunds and credits as each firm's rules give them; not taxes or payment costs), that one person receives per month in the first year of the recommended plan: 28 accounts at 13 prop firms, each traded with zero predictive skill under every rule of its firm that the model contains and the plan's own policies (flat every evening; no trade and no day above 40% of a target; at least 10 minutes between trades and no target or stop closer than 0.6 hourly standard deviations; an account near its floor traded out with a last stop at the floor, so that it ends in a breach that frees its allocation). In 90% of simulated first years the total lies between $96,538 and $1,564,866 (median $710,994).

What changed from version 7

A second detailed review, of version 7, raised 27 findings (Appendix A); fixing them, and checking the corrected engine with new tests, found 16 further errors: n1–n7 in version 7, n8–n11, n14 and n15 in version 8's own first engine and checks, and n12, n13 and n16 in both. Every rule finding was checked against the firms' own pages and every model finding against the code. The figure moves from version 7's $70,128 a month to $62,198. The changes push in both directions. Lower: at FundingPips, The5ers, FXIFY, GFT, Hola Prime, Maven and Alpha Capital the daily loss limit is a share of the day's starting balance, so it shrinks after losses, and at FunderPro the stricter of its two possible bases is used; FXIFY Classic returns no fee; Maven needs three profitable days for every payout, and the plan keeps Maven's funded profit at or below $4,900 so that its 50% rule (above $5,000) never applies; Alpha's first payout needs five trading days; FunderPro Classic has minimum trading days; no position re-enters within 10 minutes of a close; no target or stop is closer than 0.6 hourly standard deviations; and every trade is now decided, and its result counted, on the day it opens, and a payout no longer restarts the day's caps (versions 7 and 8's first engine let some days exceed the 40% cap and GFT's day cap, n12 and n13). Higher: Hola Prime's 40% rule belongs to on-demand payouts, not the bi-weekly option; Fintokei's and GFT's minimum payouts were overstated; an account near its floor is now traded out (a small chance to recover) instead of abandoned. Neither way by construction: the random numbers of every stage and market are now independent streams, the paths have one-minute sub-steps, and micro futures are valued at the path's price. Programme by programme (Chapter 9.4), 19 of 24 chosen settings are below their version 7 value by more than their interval, 0 above it, and the rest within it. The figure is a new measurement under the corrected rules, not version 7's plus a list. Version 6's $138,412 rested on bold play, which several firms forbid; version 7 replaced it with the 40% policy.

Risk / k / m / X: risk per trade and payout target as a share of the account, reward-to-risk, stop in hourly standard deviations. Per-account values are long-run rates from sixteen fresh paths; their sum ($74,751) is a different quantity from the first-year average ($62,198), see 8.5.

What the number means, and what it does not

  • It is an expected value under a model: the average over many possible years if prices are fair random walks with the measured volatility and costs. The ± is the simulation's precision, not a range for real income.
  • It assumes zero skill: the value comes from the contract (a capped loss, a share of gains), not from predicting prices.
  • It assumes the firms pay as their rules say and do not judge the plan's trading to break their behavioural rules (Chapter 31.2). The refusal scenarios above price random refusals only.
  • It assumes every slot is refilled at once, which needs cash for fees; with a finite budget the first year is lower (Chapter 28.7).
  • It is per person: every firm limits what one person may hold. Accounts in anyone else's name are not allowed and are not in the plan.

How it was found

  1. Part I derives every formula and says which model it is exact for; the exact chain of 5.5 implements the plan's actual rule near the floor. tests_v8.py checks every new rule mechanism of the engine directly (Chapter 11).
  2. Every programme's settings (risk, \(k\), stop, payout target, market) were searched over 10,857 grid settings and 397 refined ones (28 million simulated attempts), then re-measured on sixteen fresh paths each.
  3. The engine was checked against the chain with the daily rules switched off (Chapter 11), and the rules' own effect measured with them on.
  4. The accounts were run together, one person at a time, each simulated life on its own new paths: 8,400 first years, 800 three-year lives, and the budget and stress scenarios.

Notation

Notation and conventions

SymbolMeaning
\(S\)account size
\(F\)fee per attempt
\(A, A_1, A_2\)profit target of a phase
\(B\)maximum loss of a phase (distance to the floor)
\(b\)safety buffer above the floor ($100 per $100K)
\(d\)daily loss limit
\(D\)funded allowance
\(\alpha\)profit split
\(R\)fee refund
\(X_1, X\)funded payout target: first, later
\(L, l\)risk per trade: the plan's, and the one actually used
\(k\)reward-to-risk setting
\(w\)net win of a trade, after caps and costs
\(c, \rho\)round-trip cost of a trade; per dollar of risk, \(\rho = \kappa/(m\sigma)\)
\(\sigma\)hourly standard deviation of the log price
\(m\)stop distance in hourly standard deviations
\(N\)notional of a position, \(l/(m\sigma)\)
\(\kappa\)round-trip cost as a fraction of the notional
\(\lambda\)leverage (1:\(\lambda\))
\(p\)win probability of one bracket
\(P, P_1, P_2\)pass probability
\(q_1, q\)probability that a funded cycle reaches its payout target
\(X_\text{end}\)balance at which an attempt or account ends
\(V, C, T\)value of an attempt, funded cash, duration
\(K\)expected total cost of a phase or cycle
EV/month\(30.44\,\mathbb{E}[V]/\mathbb{E}[T]\) in days, a long-run rate

Money is in US dollars. A 100K account is $100,000 of simulated capital. ± is a 95% Monte Carlo interval under the model. "Fresh paths" are synthetic price paths that played no part in choosing the setting they measure. "Engine" is the rule-by-rule simulation; "chain" the exact trade-level equations; "Brownian" the continuous approximation. Tiers A, B, D are the firms' reliability classes (Chapter 29).

Part I

The mathematics, from zero

Every result in this document follows from a handful of facts about fair games. This part derives each of them, says exactly which model each formula is exact for, and works the numbers of the plan through at every step. Where a formula is an approximation it is labelled as one, and the engine (the rule-by-rule simulation) supplies the figure that is used.

Chapter 1

What is being bought

A proprietary trading firm ("prop firm") sells an evaluation. You pay a fee, you trade a simulated account under written rules, and if you meet the profit targets without breaking a loss limit, the firm gives you a funded account. On the funded account you keep a share of every profit you withdraw, and the firm absorbs every loss. You never owe the firm money beyond the fee.

That last sentence is the whole opportunity. Your downside is capped at the fee, and your upside is a share of profits that, as Chapter 7 shows, have a positive expected size even when every trade is a coin flip. The contract is an option: you pay a premium (the fee) for the right to keep part of the gains of a game whose losses fall on someone else.

1.1 The parts of the contract

SymbolMeaningFTMO 2-Step 100K, as an example
\(S\)account size (simulated money)$100,000
\(F\)fee for one attempt€540 = $607.82 at 1.1256
\(A_1, A_2\)profit targets of phase 1 and phase 2$10,000 and $5,000
\(B\)maximum loss: the floor sits \(B\) below the starting balance and never moves ("static")$10,000
\(d\)daily loss limit, measured from a reference level set at each day's reset$5,000
\(D\)maximum loss on the funded account (the "allowance")$10,000
\(\alpha\)your share ("split") of every profit withdrawn80%
\(R\)fee refund, paid with the first payout$607.82
—minimum trading days, payout cycle, other rules4 days per phase; payouts every 14 days

1.2 The value of one attempt

Call the cash an attempt brings you \(V\). It is the fee (always paid), plus, if both phases are passed, whatever the funded account pays out (the refund included):

Value of one attempt $$V = -F + \mathbf{1}\{\text{pass}\}\cdot C, \qquad \mathbb{E}[V] = -F + \Pr(\text{pass})\cdot \mathbb{E}[C \mid \text{pass}]$$

Here \(\mathbf{1}\{\text{pass}\}\) is 1 if both phases are passed and 0 otherwise, and \(C\) is the total cash from the funded account. Where a firm returns part of the fee as non-cash credit when a phase is passed (The5ers), that credit is a third term, counted separately from cash (Chapter 14).

1.3 The whole argument in five lines, in an idealised model

Before the details, the result in its simplest form: no trading costs, every phase ending exactly at its target or its floor, and payouts of a fixed cycle target \(X\) from a static allowance. Chapter 5 proves that a fair game reaches \(+A\) before \(-B\) with probability \(B/(A+B)\) in that model; Chapter 7 proves that a funded account then withdraws on average exactly \(D\) before it is lost, and reaches its first payout with probability \(q = D/(D+X)\). The refund comes only with that first payout, so it is weighted by \(q\):

FTMO 2-Step, zero cost, zero skill, idealised $$\Pr(\text{pass}) = \frac{B}{A_1+B}\cdot\frac{B}{A_2+B} = \frac{10{,}000}{20{,}000}\cdot\frac{10{,}000}{15{,}000} = \frac{1}{3}$$ $$\mathbb{E}[V] = -F + \Pr(\text{pass})\big(\alpha D + q\,R\big) = -632 + \tfrac13\Big(8{,}000 + \tfrac{10{,}000}{10{,}000 + X}\,632\Big)$$

At the version 6 fee of $632 this is $2,140.00 with a cycle of \(X = \$10{,}000\) and $2,087.33 with \(X = \$30{,}000\); version 6 printed $2,245, which counts the refund on every pass and is only the limit as \(X \to 0\). At the correct fee of $607.82 and \(X = \$10{,}000\) it is \(-607.82 + \tfrac13(8{,}000 + 303.91) = \$2{,}160.15\).

Everything else in this document refines this number: trading costs lower it; the plan's policies (flat every evening, at most 40% of a target from one trade or one day, at least 10 minutes between trades, targets and stops at least 0.6 hourly standard deviations from the entry, and a last trade with its stop at the floor when an account has too little room left) change both the value and the time; and the settings are then chosen to make the most of it per month and per person. The sign is already visible here; its size needs the full engine.

1.4 Why the firm still makes money, and what that implies

The firm's revenue is the fees of everyone, and most traders are not zero-skill traders with a plan built around the contract: they over-trade, break rules, abandon accounts and stop after one failure. A plan that treats the contract as a mathematical object and repeats it many times is the case the firm's pricing does not cover. Firms know this, and their terms contain behavioural rules (against "gambling", "all-in" trading, one-trade target attainment, account rolling) that are written broadly and applied at their discretion. The plan is built to meet every rule that can be stated as a condition on trades, and Chapter 31 lists the behavioural rules that cannot, firm by firm, as conditions under which the figures here apply.

Chapter 2

The probability toolkit

Seven ideas carry the analysis. Each is stated with the conditions it needs.

2.1 Expected value and linearity

A random quantity \(X\) that takes values \(x_1, x_2, \dots\) with probabilities \(p_1, p_2, \dots\) has expected value \(\mathbb{E}[X] = \sum_i p_i\, x_i\). For any random quantities, dependent or not,

$$\mathbb{E}[X+Y] = \mathbb{E}[X] + \mathbb{E}[Y], \qquad \mathbb{E}[cX] = c\,\mathbb{E}[X].$$

Linearity says that the expected cash of many accounts over one horizon, under one policy is the sum of their expected cash over that horizon, however strongly they move together. It does not say that a first year's average equals a long-run rate (they are different quantities, Chapter 8.5), and it does not make correlation irrelevant: correlation leaves the sum of the means alone and changes everything else (the spread, the cash needed for fees, the chance of many failures at once).

2.2 Variance

\(\operatorname{Var}(X) = \mathbb{E}[(X-\mathbb{E}X)^2] = \mathbb{E}[X^2] - (\mathbb{E}X)^2\). For independent \(X, Y\) variances add; for dependent ones a term \(2\operatorname{Cov}(X,Y)\) is added. Variance enters the time a fair game takes (Chapter 8) and the confidence intervals of every simulated figure.

2.3 Fair games and martingales

A sequence of balances \(X_0, X_1, \dots\) is a martingale if \(\mathbb{E}[X_{n+1} \mid X_0,\dots,X_n] = X_n\). "Zero skill" means this for the balance before costs: the price is a martingale, the balance is a sum of positions times price changes, and no rule for direction, size or timing that uses only the past can change that. With costs the expected next balance is lower by the cost: a supermartingale.

2.4 Stopping times and the optional stopping theorem

A stopping time \(\tau\) is a rule for when to stop that uses only information available at the time. The optional stopping theorem: if \(X\) is a martingale, \(\tau\) is almost surely finite, and the stopped process \(X_{n\wedge\tau}\) is bounded (or, more generally, uniformly integrable), then

$$\mathbb{E}[X_\tau] = X_0 .$$

Each use below states why the condition holds. For one phase it holds because the balance stays between the floor and the target plus one win. For a funded account whose profits are withdrawn the balance plus everything withdrawn is not bounded, so Chapter 7 applies the theorem to one payout cycle at a time, where it is.

2.5 Wald's identity

If the steps \(Y_1, Y_2, \dots\) are independent and identically distributed with finite mean \(\mu\), and \(\tau\) is a stopping time with \(\mathbb{E}[\tau] < \infty\), then \(\mathbb{E}[\sum_{n\le\tau} Y_n] = \mu\,\mathbb{E}[\tau]\). Having the same unconditional mean at every step is not enough: with \(Y_1 = \pm1\) equally likely, \(Y_2 = -Y_1\), and stopping after the first step if \(Y_1 = +1\) and after the second otherwise, every step has mean 0 but the stopped sum has mean \(\tfrac12\). The form used here is the martingale one: if the conditional mean of each step given the past is \(-c_n\) (a cost known when the trade is placed), the conditional absolute means are bounded and \(\mathbb{E}[\tau] < \infty\), then \(\mathbb{E}[X_\tau - X_0] = -\mathbb{E}[\sum_{n\le\tau} c_n]\), because \(X_n + \sum_{i\le n} c_i\) is a martingale with bounded increments in mean and optional stopping applies. Every trade of the plan has conditional mean minus its own cost given everything before it (4.3), so this holds even when the cost varies from trade to trade.

2.6 Brownian motion

The continuous-time limit of a fair random walk is Brownian motion \(W_t\): it starts at 0, has independent increments, and \(W_{t+h} - W_t\) is normal with mean 0 and variance \(h\). A process \(X_t = \mu t + \sigma W_t\) has three martingales that carry the approximations of Chapters 5 and 8:

$$X_t - \mu t, \qquad (X_t-\mu t)^2 - \sigma^2 t, \qquad \exp\!\left(-\tfrac{2\mu}{\sigma^2} X_t\right).$$

The third follows from Itô's formula: \(d e^{\lambda X} = e^{\lambda X}(\lambda\mu + \tfrac12\lambda^2\sigma^2)\,dt + (\dots)\,dW\), whose \(dt\) term vanishes for \(\lambda = -2\mu/\sigma^2\). These formulas are exact for Brownian motion; applied to a sum of bracket trades, which jump, they are approximations (5.2).

2.7 The reflection principle

For a Brownian path from \(a\) to \(b\) over a time with variance \(v\), the chance that it rose above \(y \ge \max(a,b)\) in between is

$$\Pr\big(\max \ge y \,\big|\, a \to b\big) = \exp\!\left(-\frac{2(y-a)(y-b)}{v}\right).$$

This gives the marginal distribution of the maximum of a bridge (and, by symmetry, of the minimum). The maximum and the minimum of one bridge are dependent, and the marginal alone does not say which of a high and a low level was touched first. Section 3.3 bounds the error of drawing them independently.

Chapter 3

The price model

3.1 Hourly volatility

Over one hour the log price changes by a random amount with standard deviation \(\sigma\), the hourly volatility, measured on two years of real hourly bars (Yahoo Finance, 2024–26, only hours that follow another trading hour):

MarketPrice, 2 Oct 2026Hourly σDaily σRound-trip cost κκ/σEntry hours used (UTC)
Nasdaq 100 CFD (US100)31,070.000.2737%1.31%0.0064%0.02301–18
USDJPY157.830.1193%0.58%0.0051%0.04300–18
EURUSD1.1260.0853%0.42%0.0062%0.07307–18
gold (XAUUSD)4,176.800.2838%1.36%0.0108%0.03801–18
micro Nasdaq futures (MNQ)31,070.000.2737%1.31%0.0027%0.01001–18

σ: standard deviation of hourly log returns on 2024–26 data (sigma_v32.json); daily = hourly × √23 (indices, gold) or √24 (currencies). κ: spread + commission + slippage allowance as a fraction of the notional, per round trip (calibrate.py). κ/σ is the cost of one hourly standard deviation of movement. Entry hours: the market's hours, cut at 19:00 UTC by the daily flat (no entry from 19:00, everything closed at 20:00).

Over \(h\) hours the standard deviation grows like \(\sigma\sqrt h\): the Nasdaq moves about 1.31% in a 23-hour trading day. Every stop in the plan is a multiple of \(\sigma\), so the same rules fit every market.

3.2 Zero drift, stated precisely

The model gives the price no expected change: \(\mathbb{E}[P_{t+h} \mid P_t] = P_t\). That is the zero-skill assumption, and it is a modelling choice, not a theorem. For the price itself to be a martingale, the log price must drift down by \(\sigma^2/2\) per hour (about 0.0004% for the Nasdaq): \(\log P_{t+h} - \log P_t\) is normal with mean \(-\sigma^2 h/2\) and variance \(\sigma^2 h\). The Nasdaq's real long-run rise (about 0.0025% per hour, a hundredth of \(\sigma\)) is left out. Version 7 justified that by saying the plan is long and short equally often; equal counts do not settle it, because the drift's effect depends on the signed exposure over each trade's holding time. In the model the direction of every account's first position is chosen by a rule that is symmetric in up and down moves, so, with the price a martingale, a real drift would add its effect to longs and subtract it from shorts in expectation; the plan's results are therefore stated for the zero-drift model, and a real drift is one of the things that could make them wrong (Chapter 31.6).

3.3 Synthetic paths

  1. Each hour is split into 60 one-minute sub-steps of normal increments of the log price with variance \(\sigma^2/60\) and mean \(-\sigma^2/120\) (version 7: 12 five-minute sub-steps).
  2. Within each sub-step, the highest and the lowest point are drawn from the exact marginal distributions of a Brownian bridge between the sub-step's end points (2.7): $$\max = \tfrac12\left(a+b+\sqrt{(b-a)^2 - 2v\ln U}\right),\qquad U\sim\text{Uniform}(0,1),$$ and the minimum is the mirror image with an independent draw. Each is exact on its own; drawing them independently is not exact jointly, because a bridge that rises far is a little less likely to fall far as well.
  3. When a stop and a target both fall inside one hourly bar, the engine replays that hour's sixty sub-steps in time order and takes the level the path touched first; it also uses the exit minute for the 10-minute gap between trades (10.4) and for the trade's duration. Only if both levels fall inside the same one-minute sub-step does it fall back to the trade's overall fair chance, which is not the right conditional probability.
  4. How large the error can be. The independent draw and the fallback differ from an exact Brownian path only on sub-steps whose path reaches both the stop and the target of an open trade. If the bracket has log-width \(W\) and a sub-step has variance \(v\), the independent draw gives that event probability at most \(e^{-2[(u-a)(u-b) + (a-\ell)(b-\ell)]/v} \le e^{-W^2/v}\) (with end points \(a, b\) inside the bracket, the exponent is smallest when both sit at its middle), and the exact bridge gives it at most the chance that the sub-step's range exceeds \(W\), which is smaller still. With one-minute sub-steps and a stop of \(m\) hourly standard deviations, \(W \ge m\sigma\) and \(v = \sigma^2/60\), so the error is at most \(e^{-60m^2}\) per minute of a trade: \(4\times10^{-10}\) at the plan's smallest stop of 0.6, \(2\times10^{-15}\) at 0.75. The engine adds this bound up over every simulated trade (Chapter 11). Within it, the construction is exact.
  5. Markets that close at weekends are closed in the path from Friday to Sunday, but the price keeps moving underneath, so Monday can open with a gap. Under the plan's daily flat (6.5) no position is open then.
  6. Random streams. Every path is drawn from its own stream of random numbers, a numpy SeedSequence keyed by the path number, the market group, the length, the sub-steps per hour and the correlation. Each stage has its own path numbers: the grid search 101–104, the refinement 111–114, the final value of every chosen setting 121–136 (sixteen paths), the sensitivities 141–144, the checks 401–6131, the rule tests 901, and the portfolio simulation one new path number per simulated life (100,000 and up; 300,000 and up for three-year lives). Gold, USDJPY and EURUSD use the life's number with their own key, so their paths are independent of the Nasdaq and of each other. Version 7 gave other markets offset numbers (+50, +60, +70), which made, for example, gold's validation paths reuse the random numbers of yen's search paths; streams_v8.py lists every stream of every stage and checks that no two independent stages share one (stream_manifest_v8.json). The Nasdaq CFD and the micro Nasdaq future share one stream on purpose: they follow the same index. Chapter 28 measures what a correlation of 0.3 between all four markets changes.

3.4 What the model leaves out

Real prices have volatility that changes (calm and wild months), fat tails, jumps at news, and spreads that widen at the daily break and around releases. The plan's stops and targets are at least 0.6 hourly standard deviations from the entry, so that average costs are a fair description and no trade is a scalp, and the cost stress tests of Chapter 31 double every cost. The synthetic model is the right tool for the question asked (what is the contract worth to a trader with no edge), not a forecast of any particular year.

Chapter 4

The bracket trade

Every trade in the plan is a bracket: one position with a stop-loss and a take-profit attached the moment it opens, never moved, and closed at the latest at 20:00 UTC the same day.

4.1 Set-up

Open a long position at price \(e\) with notional \(N\) dollars (a short is the mirror image), stop a fraction \(s\) below the entry and target a fraction \(u\) above. The plan fixes three things and derives the rest:

  • the risk \(l\): what a stopped trade loses before costs. Normally \(l = L\) (1–1.75% of the account, searched in Chapter 9), but never more than the room left, net of the trade's own cost (5.4);
  • the stop distance \(s = m\sigma\): \(m\) hourly standard deviations;
  • the win \(w\) the trade nets after costs if it reaches its target: \(w = \min\big(\max(\min(k\,l,\ \text{distance to the target}),\ w_{\min}),\ \text{caps}\big)\), where \(k\) is the reward-to-risk setting, the caps are the plan's 40% concentration policy (6.7) and any firm cap, and \(w_{\min} = l\,(0.6/m - \rho)\) keeps the take-profit at least 0.6 hourly standard deviations from the entry (a target closer than that is reached a little past it). If a cap leaves less than \(w_{\min}\): with nothing made yet that day, the risk is lowered until the cap is a full minimum-distance win (a small payout target, Maven's ceiling), never below one futures contract; with profit already made that day, no further trade until tomorrow.
Position and target $$N = \frac{l}{s}, \qquad N u - c = w \;\Rightarrow\; u = \frac{s\,(w+c)}{l}.$$

A win pays \(+w\) and a loss costs \(-(l+c)\), where \(c = \kappa N\) is the round-trip cost (spread, commission, slippage allowance) of this position.

4.2 The win probability

The price is a martingale and the trade ends at the first touch of the stop or the target. If nothing else can end it and fills are exact, the exit price is bounded, so optional stopping (2.4) gives \(p\,e(1+u) + (1-p)\,e(1-s) = e\), i.e. \(p = s/(s+u)\):

Win probability of a zero-skill bracket (fixed cost, exact fills) $$p = \frac{l}{l + w + c} = \frac{1}{1 + w/l + c/l}.$$

4.3 The mean and the variance of one trade

With outcomes \(+w\) (probability \(p\)) and \(-(l+c)\):

$$\mathbb{E}[\text{pnl}] = p\,w - (1-p)(l+c) = \frac{l\,w - (w+c)(l+c)}{l+w+c} = -c,$$ $$\operatorname{Var}(\text{pnl}) = p\,(w+c)^2 + (1-p)\,l^2 = l\,(w+c).$$

These hold for the binary trade of 4.2 with a cost known at entry. Two things in the plan make some outcomes non-binary. A trade still open at 20:00 UTC is closed at that hour's price, somewhere between stop and target; the price is still a martingale and the closing time a bounded stopping time, so the mean is still \(-c\), but the variance is smaller and the distribution has three parts. And a price that gaps through a level fills at the gap. The engine simulates both; the chain of Chapter 5 uses the binary trade.

4.4 What a trade costs

The round-trip cost is a fraction \(\kappa\) of the notional, so per dollar of risk

$$\rho \equiv \frac{c}{l} = \frac{\kappa N}{l} = \frac{\kappa}{m\sigma}.$$
Market\(\rho\) at m = 0.60.751.01.25at cost × 2, m = 0.6m = 1.0
Nasdaq 100 CFD (US100)3.90%3.12%2.34%1.87%7.79%4.68%
USDJPY7.12%5.70%4.27%3.42%14.25%8.55%
EURUSD12.12%9.70%7.27%5.82%24.24%14.55%
gold (XAUUSD)6.34%5.07%3.81%3.04%12.68%7.61%

The round-trip cost in units of the risk, \(\rho = c/l = \kappa/(m\sigma)\), for stops of \(m\) hourly standard deviations. The plan holds nothing through a daily reset, so there is no financing to add (version 6 paid up to 16% of the risk per night held at a tight Nasdaq stop).

Version 6 held winning trades through nights and paid overnight financing \(\varphi N\) per rollover, a cost that is random (it depends on how long the trade lasts) and was not in the formulas. Versions 7 and 8 hold no position through a daily reset (6.5), so there is no financing to add, and \(\rho\) is the whole cost.

4.5 The plan's trade on the Nasdaq, worked through

The plan's FTMO setting is risk \(L = \$1{,}500\) (1.50% of $100,000), a stop of \(m = 0.75\) hourly standard deviations, \(k = 8\) and a payout target of $20,000. The first trade of phase 1 aims at \(w = \min(k\,L,\ A,\ 0.4A) = \min(\$12{,}000,\ \$10{,}000,\ \$4{,}000) = \$4{,}000\). With the Nasdaq at 31,070:

Stop distance: \(s = m\sigma = 0.75 \times 0.2737\% = 0.2053\%\) of the price, i.e. \(63.8\) index points.

Notional: \(N = l/s = \$730{,}758\), i.e. \(\$23.52\) per index point. If one lot is worth $1 per point (read the contract specification), \(23.52\) lots, rounded down.

Cost: \(c = \kappa N = 0.0064\% \times \$730{,}758 = \$46.77\), so \(\rho = c/l = 3.12\%\).

Target: \(u = s(w+c)/l = 0.2053\% \times 4{,}046.77/1{,}500 = 0.5538\%\), i.e. \(172.1\) points from the entry.

Win probability: \(p = l/(l+w+c) = 1{,}500/(1{,}500 + 4{,}000 + 46.77) = 0.2704\).

Mean \(-c = -\$46.77\); check: \(p\,w - (1-p)(l+c) = -46.77\). Variance \(l(w+c) = 6{,}070{,}153\), standard deviation \(\$2{,}464\).

Margin at 1:50: \(N/50 = \$14{,}615\) = 14.6% of the starting balance; the engine re-checks it against the current balance at every entry (10.2).

Duration (8.1, approximate): \(m^2 (w+c)/l = 1.52\) hours of open market on average; losers \(1.20\) h, winners \(2.38\) h. A trade still open at 20:00 UTC is closed there.

On the funded account (payout target $20,000) a full win is \(\min(k\,L, X, 0.4X) = \$8{,}000\): take-profit 342 points away, \(p = 0.1571\), winners 7.4 hours of open market on average, so some are cut by the daily flat and some wait for the next day's cap. No take-profit is ever closer than 0.6 hourly standard deviations (51 points): a target closer than that is reached a little past it.

4.6 Ways a simulation can flatter a trade

  1. Skipping the entry bar. Checking levels only from the next hour ignores stop-outs inside the entry hour. (Measured in version 4: +0.14 R per trade, all fake.)
  2. Sampling prices too rarely. A few prices an hour miss touches in between, and the nearer level (the stop) is missed more often. The bridge extremes of 3.3 remove it.
  3. Ties inside one bar. When one bar's range contains both levels, the bar alone does not say which came first. Version 5 used the fair chance; versions 6 and 7 replay five-minute sub-steps; version 8 replays one-minute sub-steps, and the remaining error is bounded in 3.3.
  4. Gaps. A price that opens beyond the stop fills at the open. Under the daily flat there is no overnight or weekend position to gap.
  5. A rounding error at the target. A win sized to close the gap to the target lands a few billionths of a dollar short in floating point; version 6's exact comparison then placed a pointless extra trade (risking $1,500 to make $1). Since version 7 the engine compares with a one-cent tolerance.
  6. Re-entering at once. Checking only whole hours lets a trade close at 12:59 and the next open at 13:00. The engine uses the exit minute and waits at least 10 minutes (10.4).
Chapter 5

Reaching a target before a floor

A phase is passed when the balance gains \(A\) before it loses \(B\): the gambler's ruin. This chapter solves it in an idealised model, then exactly for the plan's actual rule.

5.1 The idealised model: \(P = B/(A+B)\)

Let \(X_n\) be the balance after \(n\) trades, a martingale with \(X_0 = 0\), and \(\tau\) the first time it reaches \(+A\) or \(-B\). If every trade is sized so the balance lands exactly on one of the two, \(X_\tau\) is \(+A\) with probability \(P\) and \(-B\) otherwise, and optional stopping gives

$$0 = \mathbb{E}[X_\tau] = P\,A - (1-P)\,B \;\;\Rightarrow\;\; P = \frac{B}{A+B}.$$

The exact-landing assumption is what this formula needs. Under the plan's rule every failure does end exactly on the floor (5.4), but a pass can end a little above the target (the minimum target distance of 4.1 and the $20 filler margin of 6.3), so the formula is the benchmark and 5.5 gives the exact value.

5.2 With costs, approximately: the Brownian formula

Treat the balance as a Brownian motion with drift \(-c\) and variance \(v = l(w+c)\) per trade. By 2.6, \(e^{\theta X}\) with \(\theta = 2c/v\) is a martingale, and optional stopping gives

Pass probability with costs (Brownian approximation) $$P \approx \frac{e^{\theta B} - 1}{e^{\theta (A+B)} - 1}, \qquad \theta = \frac{2c}{v} = \frac{2\rho}{l\,(w/l + \rho)},\qquad P \approx \frac{B}{A+B}\left(1 - \frac{c\,A}{v}\right).$$

This is accurate when each trade is small next to \(A\) and \(B\) and its size does not change. The plan's trades are capped (40% of the target), shrink near the floor and the daily limit, and jump: the exact chain of 5.5 is used instead, and the Brownian value is shown only for comparison.

5.3 How many trades, approximately

With a constant trade, \((X_n)^2 - n v\) is a martingale before costs, so \(\mathbb{E}[\tau] = \mathbb{E}[X_\tau^2]/v = AB/v\) in the idealised model. With trades that change size the relevant quantity is the accumulated variance \(\sum_n v(X_n)\), not \(v\) times the count; the chain computes the expected count exactly.

5.4 The plan's rule near the floor

Version 6's written rule could leave an account a few dollars above its floor with no trade ever allowed. Version 7 ended the attempt there and bought a replacement; but an account that has not breached is still open, and at firms with a per-person limit (FTMO's $400,000, FXIFY's one account per size) it may still count against it. Version 8 ends every account in a way the firm records: a breach. At a balance \(x\) (relative to the start), with floor \(-B\), buffer \(b\) ($100 per $100K) and the cost per dollar of risk \(\rho\):

Risk of the next trade, and the terminal trade $$\text{room}(x) = \frac{x + B - b}{1+\rho}, \qquad l(x) = \min\big(L,\ \text{room}(x),\ \text{today's room},\ \text{margin room}\big) \quad\text{if room}(x) \ge l_{\min} = \max(0.1\,L,\ \text{one futures contract}),$$ $$\text{otherwise (the terminal trade):}\quad l(x) = \min\Big(\frac{x + B}{1+\rho},\ \text{today's room}\Big)\ \text{(at least the smallest lot; one contract on futures), with its stop at the floor itself.}$$

On futures \(L\) in the first line is at least one contract: where one contract at the planned stop risks more than \(L\), the plan trades one contract if the rooms allow it (Appendix A, n11).

A normal trade loses at most \(l(1+\rho) \le x + B - b\), so it never reaches the floor. A terminal trade is an ordinary bracket at the plan's reward:risk, small (under a tenth of the normal risk), whose stop sits at the floor: a loss ends the account in a breach, which the firm closes, freeing the allocation; a win continues it, and the account returns to normal trades once the room allows. So every failure ends exactly at \(-B\), the text, the engine and the chain follow the same rule, and no account is left open and unused. (When only today's room is too small, the account waits for tomorrow; today's room resets, the static room does not.)

5.5 The exact chain

The balance after each trade is a Markov chain on \([-B, A]\), plus a small overshoot above \(A\). At \(x\) the next trade has risk \(l(x)\) as in 5.4, win \(w(x) = \min(\max(\min(k\,l,\ A - x),\ w_{\min},\ \$1),\ \text{cap})\) (4.1), cost \(c(x) = \rho\,l(x)\) and win chance \(p(x) = l/(l + w + c)\), and moves to \(x + w\) (a pass if \(x + w \ge A\), possibly above \(A\)) or to \(\max(x - l - c, -B)\) (a failure at \(-B\) after a terminal trade). Four functions solve the same recursion with different end values:

One step of the exact chain $$V(x) = p\,V(x+w) + (1-p)\,V(x-l-c), \qquad V = 1 \text{ from } A \text{ up},\ 0 \text{ at } -B,$$ $$N(x) = 1 + p\,N(x+w) + (1-p)\,N(x-l-c), \qquad K(x) = c(x) + p\,K(x+w) + (1-p)\,K(x-l-c),$$ $$T(x) = p\,T(x+w) + (1-p)\,T(x-l-c), \qquad T = \text{the balance where the attempt ends}.$$

\(V\) is the pass probability, \(N\) the expected number of trades, \(K\) the expected total cost and \(T\) the expected final balance. They are solved by value iteration on a grid of about 8,000 points (analytic_v8.py). Because every trade's conditional mean is exactly minus its own cost (4.3), the stopped balance is bounded and the number of trades has finite mean, optional stopping (2.4, 2.5) gives the accounting identity, which the solver checks:

The accounting identity, exact $$P\,\mathbb{E}[X_{\text{end}} \mid \text{pass}] + (1-P)\,\mathbb{E}[X_{\text{end}} \mid \text{fail}] = -K(0), \qquad\text{and with every failure at } -B:\quad P = \frac{B - K(0)}{\mathbb{E}[X_{\text{end}} \mid \text{pass}] + B}.$$

The textbook form \(P = (B - K)/(A+B)\) is the special case where every pass ends exactly at \(A\). Under the plan's rule a pass ends a little above \(A\), so the textbook form overstates \(P\) slightly (0.04 to 1.93 percentage points over the chosen programmes).

FTMO phase 1 (\(A = B = \$10{,}000\), buffer $100) at the plan's setting (risk $1,500, \(k = 8\), \(m = 0.75\), \(\rho = 3.12\%\)). No win can exceed \(0.4A = \$4{,}000\), so no single win ends the phase from the start; the first trades along the all-loss path:

Trade \(j\) on the all-loss pathBalance \(x_j\)Risk \(l_j\)Cost \(c_j\)Win \(w_j\)\(p_j\)A win ends the phase?
1$0$1,500$46.77$4,0000.2704no

4−$4,640$1,500$46.77$4,0000.2704no

Chain (5.5): \(P_1 = 0.4575\), \(\mathbb{E}[\text{trades}] = 17.81\), \(K(0) = \$793\), \(\mathbb{E}[X_\text{end}\mid\text{fail}] = -\$10{,}000\).

Identity: \(P_1\,\mathbb{E}[X_\text{end}\mid\text{pass}] + (1-P_1)(-B) = 0.4575\times10{,}125 - 0.5425\times10{,}000 = -792.66 = -K(0)\) (gap +0.0000); the mean balance at a pass is $10,125, above \(A\) by the $20 filler margin and the small overshoot of minimum-distance targets.

Exact form \(P = (B - K)/(\mathbb{E}[X_\text{end}\mid\text{pass}] + B) = 0.4575\); the textbook form \((B - K)/(A + B) = 0.4599\) (with \(A\) the target the engine trades to, the firm's target plus the $20 filler margin of 6.3) is 0.24 percentage points higher, because a pass ends a little above \(A\). Every failure ends exactly at \(-B\) under the terminal rule (5.4).

Brownian approximation (5.2) with a constant full-size trade: \(P_1 \approx 0.4616\).

Phase 2 (\(A = \$5{,}000\), wins capped at $2,000): \(P_2 = 0.5972\), 18.76 trades, \(K = \$846\). Both: \(P = 0.2732\) (chain).

The engine (with the daily limit, the daily flat, minimum days, the per-day concentration cap and weekends), on sixteen fresh paths: \(P = 0.2730 \pm 0.0056\). With those rules switched off (11.2) it gives 0.2711 ± 0.0099, against the chain's 0.2737.

5.6 Bold play, and why the plan does not use it

Each trade costs \(c\) on average, so a phase's pass probability falls with its expected total cost (5.5). The way to pay the least cost is to need the fewest trades: stake enough to reach the target in one win. This is bold play, which Dubins and Savage (1965) proved optimal for reaching a goal in a fair or slightly unfavourable game. Version 6's search found it: left free, every programme moved to \(k\) of 20–30 and payout cycles of 25–30% of the account, so that a single trade could pass a phase or fill a payout.

Version 7 gave that up, deliberately, and version 8 keeps it so. Several firms forbid exactly this behaviour: Fintokei forbids reaching a target "in one or a few identical trades", The5ers bans "one-trade target attainment" permanently, Alpha forbids "all or nothing" trading, FundingPips' Profit Concentration Policy reacts to one trade idea above 60% of a target, and FundedNext, Hola Prime, FTMO and FXIFY reserve the right to treat concentrated outcomes as gambling. The plan therefore caps every trade and every day at 40% of the current target (6.7), so that every phase takes at least three winning days (a payout taken on its date before the target can rest on fewer, 6.7). Under that cap one win can never end a phase, the product formula for bold play (the chance of passing as a sequence of all-or-nothing trades) never applies, and the chain of 5.5 is the exact value.

For reference, the bold product formula, \(P = \sum_j p_j \prod_{i<j}(1-p_i)\) with \(x_{j+1} = x_j - l_j - c_j\), is exact only if every possible win ends the phase, i.e. \(k\,l(x_j) \ge A - x_j\) at every state on the all-loss path; \(k L \ge A + B\) is not enough, because \(l(x)\) shrinks near the floor. Version 6's tables printed wins of \(A - x_j\) even where \(k\,l(x_j)\) was smaller; those rows described trades the plan would not place.

Exact chain, FTMO 2-Step 100K at the plan's stop m = 0.75, risk $1,500 and payout target $20,000. Without the cap the value keeps rising with \(k\) toward bold play; with it, \(k\) beyond the cap changes nothing.

In the engine, at the plan's setting: $2,512 ± $217 a month with the 40% policy, $3,336 ± $937 with a 60% cap and $4,077 ± $847 with no cap at the same \(k\) (paths 141–144). Version 6's uncapped FTMO setting (k = 20, m = 0.5, X = 30%) was worth $5,463 a month in its own final run.

Chapter 6

The evaluation in full

6.1 Two phases

Always, \(\Pr(\text{pass both}) = P_1 \cdot \Pr(\text{pass 2} \mid \text{passed 1})\). In the simulation's model the second phase starts on a fresh account and its trades are independent of the first's, so the conditional probability equals the unconditional \(P_2\) and the product \(P_1P_2\) holds. On real markets a volatile month that carries from one phase into the next could link them; zero edge alone does not rule that out, and the product is a model statement, not a theorem about markets. The engine simulates the two phases in sequence on one path, so whatever its path carries over is included.

Version 6 also said that putting the larger target first saves time and cost. It does not in general: with the Brownian trade count of 5.3 and targets \(A, C\) over a common \(B\), the expected number of trades is \(AB/v + \frac{B}{A+B}\cdot CB/v\), which for \(A = 10{,}000\), \(C = 5{,}000\), \(B = 10{,}000\) and \(v = 1{,}500^2 \times 5\) is 11.11 trades, against 10.37 with the targets reversed. The claim is withdrawn.

6.2 The daily loss limit

Most firms add a floor that is reset every day: the account may not fall more than \(d\) below a reference level fixed at the reset. Firms fix that level differently:

FirmReference fixed at the resetResetAllowanceEngine
FTMObalance at the reset00:00 Prague (22:00/23:00 UTC)5% of the initial balancefixed
FundingPipshigher of the day's opening balance and equityplatform midnight, UTC+3 (21:00 UTC)4% of that levelrelative
The5ershigher of the previous day's closing balance and equity00:00 server time5% of that level ($5,500 at $110,000)relative
FXIFYprevious day's balance17:00 New York4% of that balancerelative
FundedNextthe day's starting balance00:00 server time5% of the initial balancefixed
Fintokeiequity at the start of the day00:00 server time5% of that equityrelative
Hola Primeprevious day's closing balance17:00 New York5% of that balancerelative
Alpha Capitalthe day's starting balance (close of the daily candle)00:00 broker time5% of that balancerelative
GFThigher of balance and equity17:00 New York5% of that levelrelative
FunderProthe day's starting balance (balance-based)00:00 GMT+35%; base not statedthe stricter of the two
BrightFundedhigher of balance and equity at rolloverrollover, Prague time5% of the original sizefixed
Blue Guardianhigher of balance and equity17:00 New York4% of the initial balancefixed
Mavenhigher of equity and balance00:00 UTC4% of that level (4% of $1,100 in its example)relative

As published (4 October 2026; rules_snapshot_v8.json quotes the pages). Engine: 'fixed' subtracts a share of the initial balance from the reference; 'relative' a share of the day's reference itself, recomputed at every reset (version 7 used 'fixed' everywhere but Fintokei). Every reset falls between 20:00 UTC and 00:00 UTC, while the plan holds no position, so balance and equity are equal whenever a reference is fixed.

Two things differ by firm: the reference fixed at the reset (balance, equity, the higher of the two, the previous day's close) and the base of the percentage. At FTMO, FundedNext, BrightFunded and Blue Guardian the allowance is a fixed share of the initial balance, subtracted from the day's reference. At FundingPips, The5ers, FXIFY, GFT, Hola Prime, Maven, Alpha Capital and Fintokei it is the same share of the day's reference itself: The5ers' own example is a $100,000 account at $110,000 of equity at the reset, whose loss allowance for the day becomes $5,500; a FundingPips account starting a day at $94,000 has $3,760, not $4,000. FunderPro says only that its 5% is balance-based at the start of the day; the engine takes the stricter of the two bases there. Version 7 used the initial balance at every firm except Fintokei, and its response said that being flat at the reset made the engine's limit the firm's. Being flat makes balance and equity equal at the reset; it does not change the base. Version 8 recomputes each firm's allowance at every reset with its own base (acct_mc._dll_at).

With an open position at the reset, an equity-based or higher-of reference rises with the open profit, and a smooth reversal can then break the day's limit with no gap at all. The plan is flat at every reset (6.5), so the reference is the balance. Within the day, every trade's risk plus its cost fits the room left (5.4); after two or three losses the account waits for the next day.

6.3 Minimum trading days: the joint condition

FTMO (4), FundedNext (5), BrightFunded (5), FXIFY Classic (4), FunderPro Classic (4), Alpha (3), Hola Prime (3) and Fintokei (3) require days with at least one trade in each phase; Fintokei also before every payout, and Alpha five before its first. A phase is passed when the target is met and the days are complete. If the target is reached first, the plan stops trading the strategy and places one minimal filler trade (smallest lot, held at least 2 minutes, stop at most $3 per $100K) on each of the next trading days not yet traded, found on the market's real calendar (version 7 added 1.4 calendar days per missing day, an average: a target reached on a Friday needs its next filler on Monday). The target the engine trades to is the firm's target plus $20 per $100K, and at most five fillers with stops of $3 cannot take the balance back under the firm's target unless a price gaps through a stop. At Alpha's funded accounts minimal-lot fillers are not allowed; there the plan places at most one normal trade a day until it has five trading days. Fillers are not used where a rule counts profitable days (6.4).

6.4 Profitable-day rules

The5ers, Maven, Blue Guardian, GFT (whose days count only with +0.5%) and FundingPips' 95% option require days with at least +0.5% profit in each phase; FundingPips' 95% option, GFT (4), Hola Prime, Maven and, in its stricter reading, Blue Guardian also in each payout cycle. (Version 7 also applied +0.5% to Fintokei, whose rule counts any trading day.) While such days are missing, the plan trades normally until the day's profit reaches

$$g_{\text{today}} = \max\!\left(0.55\%\,S,\; \frac{\text{distance left to the target}}{\text{profitable days still needed}}\right)$$

and then stops for the day; every trade is a full fair bracket whose take-profit is capped at what is left of today's target (but never closer than the minimum distance of 4.1). Stopping at a daily target is a stopping time, so in a cost-free fair game the pass probability is unchanged; with costs, every extra trade costs \(c\), so these rules lower the value as well as stretching the time. The engine measures their effect in every programme's chapter. Under Maven's funded ceiling (profit kept at or below $4,900, 7.4) the plan also keeps room for the days still needed: every win leaves 0.55% of the account below the ceiling for each profitable day still needed after today, and for today as well if the win would leave today short of +0.5%; otherwise it is cut, or the plan waits for tomorrow. Without that reserve an account can reach the ceiling a profitable day short and never be paid (Appendix A, n10).

6.5 The daily flat, weekends and news

The plan opens no position from 19:00 UTC and closes every position at the 20:00 UTC open, every weekday. Every daily reset in the plan falls between 20:00 and 00:00 UTC (FTMO and BrightFunded near midnight Prague time, GFT and Blue Guardian at 5 pm New York, Fintokei and Maven at 00:00 UTC), so the account is flat at each of them. This also removes overnight financing, overnight and weekend gaps, and the widened spreads of the daily break. The cost is time: a trade still open at 20:00 is closed between its stop and its target (a fair outcome) and the next one waits until the next day's open. Such a trade is decided at that open, with that day's loss allowance and caps: every daily figure in the engine (loss, profit, profitable and trading days) belongs to the day a trade opens, and a payout requested during a day does not restart that day's caps (Appendix A, n12 and n13).

News. Restricted news windows differ by firm (Maven: no opening or closing within 2 minutes of a red-folder release, in evaluations too; FundingPips: profits from 5 minutes before to 5 minutes after may be deducted; FTMO funded: no new orders within 2 minutes; FundedNext: 40% of news profit counted on funded accounts). A bracket opened earlier can still reach its stop or target inside a window. The plan closes positions before any release that a firm restricts for the account's stage and market (US, euro-area and Japanese releases alike) and opens nothing until the window has passed. The engine has no news calendar, so the headline is a model without news restrictions. Chapter 28 runs a stylised news-hours sensitivity in which every account is flat from 12:00 to 14:00 UTC (and 08:00 to 09:00 on EURUSD) every weekday; it is a scenario, not a bound: those hours do not contain every restricted release, and removing trading hours has no proven one-way effect on cash per month (it changes which trades happen, when days qualify and when payouts fall).

6.6 Review time

Firms take one to three working days to check a passed phase and issue the next account. The engine adds one day after phase 1 and the firm's stated time after phase 2.

6.7 The concentration policy

On every account the plan caps the win of every trade at 40% of the current target (the phase target in an evaluation, the payout target on a funded account), and stops for the day once the day's profit reaches 40% of that target. Three consequences follow:

  • every phase needs at least three winning days, so no pass rests on one trade or one day. A funded cycle that reaches its target needs three too, but a payout taken on its date before the target can rest on fewer: where a firm requires profitable days per payout, that is a rule of the programme (6.4), not a consequence of the cap;
  • FundingPips' Profit Concentration Policy (60% of a phase target from one trade idea) can never trigger, and FXIFY's 25% best-day rule is met by its own tighter cap;
  • the effective reward-to-risk is capped: in phase 2 of a 5% target on 100K a win is at most $2,000, so at 1.75% risk at most 1.14 : 1. The search (Chapter 9) therefore covers smaller \(k\) and the risk per trade.

Chapter 9 measures what the policy costs against the uncapped strategy of version 6, programme by programme.

Chapter 7

What a funded account pays

On the funded account the plan trades each payout cycle to a profit target \(X\) above the starting balance, stops, requests the payout on the first allowed day, and withdraws everything above the start. The balance is back at the start, the static floor is still \(D\) below it, and the next cycle begins. If a payout date comes before the target, the plan withdraws whatever profit there is (where the firm's rules allow) and starts a new cycle; the cycle formulas below describe the simpler policy of paying only at the target, and the engine simulates the plan's. The account ends in a breach at the floor under the rule of 5.4 (or, at a few firms, after a fixed number of payouts).

7.1 The withdrawal identity, with its conditions

Take one cycle. Its balance (relative to the start) is a stopped sum of trades, bounded between \(-D\) and \(X\) plus one win, and each trade has conditional mean minus its own cost, so optional stopping applies to it (2.5). Let cycle \(i\) end with balance \(Z_i\) after costs \(K_i\), and let \(Q_i \ge 0\) be any profit removed from the account without being paid to the trader or counted as a cost (a firm's deduction of profit above a day cap, profit voided by a rolling cap). Then \(\mathbb{E}[Z_i] = -\mathbb{E}[K_i] - \mathbb{E}[Q_i]\). The account's total withdrawal is \(W = \sum_i Z_i\,\mathbf{1}\{\text{cycle } i \text{ paid}\}\) and it ends with balance \(X_{\text{end}}\) in its last cycle. Adding the cycles (the number of cycles has finite mean because each fails with a probability bounded away from zero):

The withdrawal identity, general form $$\mathbb{E}[W] = -\,\mathbb{E}[X_{\text{end}}] - \mathbb{E}[\text{total funded cost}] - \mathbb{E}[Q].$$

External credits (The5ers' hub credit, refunds) are paid outside the account and are added separately. Under the plan \(Q = 0\) in the model: wins are capped at what is left of GFT's daily cap and of Maven's ceiling and 30-day window, and no position is open through a gap, so nothing is deducted or voided. (In practice a take-profit filled a few points better than its price at an exact cap gives up those few points.) Under the terminal rule of 5.4 every account ends at the floor, \(X_\text{end} = -D\), and the identity becomes \(\mathbb{E}[W] = D - \mathbb{E}[\text{cost}]\), the form version 6 stated without its conditions. It fails when the account can end elsewhere: a daily-limit breach part-way down, a closure after a fixed number of payouts (Apex), or a floor that moves (Topstep, Apex); for those accounts only the engine's figures are used. The cycle-by-cycle argument is needed because the balance plus withdrawals is not bounded.

7.2 Cycle by cycle

Each cycle is a fresh run of the chain of 5.5 from 0 to its target \(X_i\) before the abandonment level, with success chance \(q_i\). Cycles continue until one fails, so cycle \(j\) is paid with probability \(\prod_{i\le j} q_i\). With a first cycle \(X_1\) and later ones \(X\) (chances \(q_1, q\)):

Funded cash, cycle form $$\mathbb{E}[C] = \alpha\left(X_1 q_1 + X\,\frac{q_1\,q}{1-q}\right) + R\cdot q_1 q^{\,r-1},$$

where the refund \(R\) comes with the \(r\)-th payout (\(r=1\) at most firms; Blue Guardian 4, Maven 3; FundingPips Flex, FXIFY Classic and BrightFunded without its paid add-on return no fee, and none is assumed at Alpha Pro); a refund in \(r\) equal parts with the first \(r\) payouts (Hola Prime) contributes \(\frac{R}{r}\sum_{j=1}^{r} q_1 q^{\,j-1}\). With two special first cycles (GFT: the first two payouts are capped, so the first two cycles have target \(X_a\) and chance \(q_a\), later ones \(X_b\), \(q_b\)):

Two special first cycles (GFT) $$\mathbb{E}[\text{paid cycles}] = q_a + q_a^2 + \frac{q_a^2\,q_b}{1-q_b} = q_a + \frac{q_a^2}{1-q_b}, \qquad \mathbb{E}[W] = X_a\,(q_a + q_a^2) + X_b\,\frac{q_a^2\,q_b}{1-q_b}.$$

(Version 6 printed \(q_1/(1-q)\) for GFT, the one-special-cycle formula; its number came from the correct one.) In the zero-cost idealised model \(q = D/(X+D)\), \(\mathbb{E}[\text{paid cycles}] = D/X\) and \(\mathbb{E}[W] = D\), which is where the zero-cost example of 1.3 takes \(q\) from.

7.3 What the cycle target changes

In the idealised zero-cost model, nothing about the average. With costs, at fixed \(l\) and \(k\), the expected number of funded trades grows with \(X\): by 5.3 it is about \(XD/v\) per cycle times \((X+D)/X\) expected cycles, i.e. \(D(X+D)/v\). So a larger cycle means more trades and more cost over the account's life, and more time between payouts; a smaller one means more payout requests and more waiting for payout dates. Version 6 said larger cycles mean fewer trades and less cost; that contradicted this formula and is withdrawn. With caps, daily targets and payout calendars the balance between the two effects is found by search (Chapter 9), and the measured effect of \(X\) is shown in each programme's chapter.

7.4 Funded rules and what they change

  • Best-day rules. If no day may exceed a share \(\beta\) of a payout's profit, each day's take-profit is capped at \(\beta X\) less what the day has made (FXIFY 25%, measured against the account's best day ever; the plan's own 40% everywhere), and a payout is requested only when the rule holds.
  • Maven: above $5,000 of profit, no "day" (a run of trades without a 24-hour gap) and no trade may exceed 50% of the cycle's profit. Under daily trading a week is one such day, so the plan keeps every Maven account's profit at or below $4,900, $100 under the threshold so that a take-profit filled a few points better than its price cannot bring the rule in, and the rule never applies. At most $10,000 of profit may be closed in any 30 days, the excess voided, so no win is placed that could take any window above it.
  • Profitable days per cycle (FundingPips 95%: 3; Hola Prime: 3; GFT: 4; Maven: 3; Blue Guardian, stricter reading: 3) and trading days (Fintokei: 3 per payout; Alpha: 5 before the first): the daily target of 6.4, or filler days of 6.3 (not at Alpha).
  • Payout calendars. The first payout is allowed 14 to 30 days after the first trade on the funded account (FTMO, FundingPips, Fintokei, GFT, Alpha and Maven count from the first trade; the engine does so for every firm, which for the others is at most hours later than funding), then every 10 to 30 days. A cycle that reaches its target early waits, flat.
  • Minimum payouts: FundingPips 1% of the account, FXIFY $50, Fintokei, GFT, FunderPro, Blue Guardian and Alpha $100, The5ers $150.
  • Payout caps (GFT: the first two payouts at most 6% or $10,000): the first two cycles aim at the cap.
  • Daily profit caps (GFT: $3,000 a day at every size): the take-profit is capped at what is left of the day's cap, so nothing is deducted.

Without costs, rules that only cap or delay wins leave the average withdrawal unchanged; with costs, each extra trade costs \(c\). The engine includes all of them.

7.5 The plan's funded account, worked through

FTMO's funded account at the plan's setting: allowance \(D = \$10{,}000\) (static), payout target \(X = \$20{,}000\), wins capped at \(0.4X = \$8{,}000\), split 80%, refund \(R = \$607.82\) with the first payout.

One cycle is the chain of 5.5 from 0 to \(X\): \(q = 0.3064\), with \(17.9\) trades per cycle on average.

\(\mathbb{E}[\text{paid cycles}] = q/(1-q) = 0.4417\); \(\mathbb{E}[W] = \mathbb{E}[X_\text{paid}]\,q/(1-q) = \$8{,}871\), where a paid cycle withdraws its target plus any overshoot.

General identity (7.1): \(-\mathbb{E}[X_\text{end}] - \mathbb{E}[\text{cost}] = 10{,}000 - 1{,}129 = \$8{,}871\) (gap +0.00). Under the terminal rule every account ends at the floor, so \(\mathbb{E}[X_\text{end}] = -D\) and the static form \(D - \mathbb{E}[\text{cost}]\) holds again; no profit is forfeited under the plan (\(Q = 0\)).

\(\mathbb{E}[C] = \alpha\,\mathbb{E}[W] + R\,q = 0.8\times8{,}871 + 607.82\times0.3064 = \$7{,}283\) (chain).

Paid cyclesProbabilityCash (split + refund)

Chain values. The cash of a funded account is lumpy: many pay nothing, a few pay several cycles.

The engine, on fresh paths, pays $7,528 per funded account (the chain with the daily rules switched off in the engine: $7,577 ± $424).

Chapter 8

Time, and value per month

8.1 How long one trade lasts, approximately

For a driftless arithmetic Brownian motion with variance \(\sigma^2\) per hour, \(Z_t^2 - \sigma^2 t\) is a martingale, and stopping at \(+d_t\) or \(-d_s\) gives \(\mathbb{E}[\tau] = d_s d_t/\sigma^2\). For the bracket, \(d_s = m\sigma\) and \(d_t = m\sigma(w+c)/l\):

Expected duration of a bracket (hours of open market), approximation $$\mathbb{E}[\tau] \approx m^2\,\frac{w + c}{l}, \qquad \mathbb{E}[\tau \mid \text{win}] \approx \frac{m^2}{3}\Big(\big(\tfrac{w+c}{l}\big)^2 + 2\,\tfrac{w+c}{l}\Big), \qquad \mathbb{E}[\tau \mid \text{loss}] \approx \frac{m^2}{3}\Big(1 + 2\,\tfrac{w+c}{l}\Big).$$

The synthetic price is geometric: its log has drift \(-\sigma^2/2\) per hour, and the barriers sit at log distances \(-\ln(1-s)\) and \(\ln(1+u)\), not \(s\) and \(u\). For the plan's brackets (\(s\) and \(u\) a fraction of a percent to a few percent) the difference is of the order of \(s\) and \(u\) themselves, a fraction of a percent of the duration. The daily flat also cuts long trades at 20:00 UTC. So these formulas are used only to explain orders of magnitude; every duration reported in Part II is measured by the engine.

8.2 How long a phase lasts

In the idealised model the number of trades is \(AB/v\) (5.3) with \(v = l(w+c)\), and each lasts about \(m^2(w+c)/l\) hours, so the market time is about \((A/l)(B/l)\,m^2\) hours, independent of \(k\). For FTMO phase 1 at \(m = 0.75\) and \(l = \$1{,}500\): \(6.67^2 \times 0.56 \approx 25\) hours of open market. The calendar is set by the rules instead: minimum and profitable days, the concentration policy's three winning days, the daily flat, weekends, reviews and payout dates.

8.3 Value per month: the renewal–reward theorem

The plan runs slots: one account position at a firm, refilled with a new evaluation as soon as an attempt ends. A slot completes attempts with values \(V_i\) and durations \(T_i\). If those pairs were independent and identically distributed, the renewal–reward theorem would give the long-run rate directly:

Renewal–reward theorem $$\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\sum_{i=1}^n V_i}{\sum_{i=1}^n T_i} = \frac{\mathbb{E}[V]}{\mathbb{E}[T]}, \qquad \text{EV per month} = 30.44\times\frac{\mathbb{E}[V]}{\mathbb{E}[T\ \text{in days}]}.$$

They are not quite: a new attempt starts on the weekday and at the hour where the last one ended, and at a few firms it carries a credit (The5ers) or a rolling window (Maven). Resetting the balance does not reset the calendar. What holds instead is a Markov renewal argument. Let \(S_i\) be the state at the start of attempt \(i\): the position in the week (a finite set at the engine's hourly resolution), plus the carried credit or window where a firm has one. Given \(S_i\), the attempt depends only on price increments no earlier attempt has seen, which in the model are independent of the past, so \((S_i)\) is a Markov chain and \((V_i, T_i)\) depend on the past only through \(S_i\). For the calendar part the chain is finite and, because attempt lengths are spread over many hours, irreducible and aperiodic, with a stationary distribution \(\pi\); the Markov renewal–reward theorem then gives the long-run rate \(\mathbb{E}_\pi[V]/\mathbb{E}_\pi[T]\), the ratio of the stationary means. Each slot's figure in Part II is an estimate of this ratio: attempts start at random points of each path, and the cluster interval of 8.4 covers the dependence of attempts on one path. (Credits and windows enlarge the state space but not the argument; the engine carries them.) This is a long-run rate: a ratio of expectations, not the expectation of ratios, and the objective each programme's settings are chosen for.

8.4 How precise a simulated figure is

With \(n\) independent attempts the ratio estimate \(\hat r = \bar V/\bar T\) has, by the delta method, standard error \(\operatorname{sd}(V_i - \hat r\,T_i)/(\sqrt{n}\,\bar T)\). Attempts that start at random times on the same synthetic path share its history, so they are not fully independent. The final figures therefore use 16 independent paths per programme (1,500 attempts on each) and treat each path as a cluster:

Cluster-robust interval for a ratio $$\widehat{\operatorname{Var}}(\hat r) = \frac{G}{G-1}\,\frac{\sum_{g=1}^{G}\big(V_g - \hat r\,T_g\big)^2}{\big(\sum_g T_g\big)^2},$$

where \(V_g, T_g\) are the totals on path \(g\). With only \(G\) clusters the multiplier for a 95% interval is the Student \(t\) quantile with \(G-1\) degrees of freedom, not 1.96: 2.13 for 16 paths, 3.18 for 4. Every "±" for a programme is the larger of that cluster interval and 1.96 attempt-level standard errors. The checks of Chapter 11 use 24 independent paths per programme for the same reason: with thousands of attempts on one path, the same stretch of price history is traded dozens of times, and an attempt-level interval would be several times too narrow. Portfolio lives each run on their own new path, so they are independent and the ordinary standard error applies. These are Monte Carlo intervals under the model: they say how well the model's expectation is estimated, not how much a real year can differ from it, and they carry no allowance for wrong rules, wrong costs or a wrong model.

8.5 A first year is not the long-run rate

A new portfolio pays every fee in its first weeks and receives its first payouts a month or more later; at the end of any finite horizon some accounts are part-way through and hold value not yet paid. So the first year's average cash, the average of later months, and the long-run renewal rate are three different quantities, and nothing requires them to be equal. Chapter 28 reports them separately: the first-year average from 12-month lives, and the average of months 13–36 and the value of the accounts still open at month 12 from 36-month lives. The months 13–36 average is a finite-window estimate: Chapter 28.6 compares its two annual windows with a paired interval before reading it as the long-run rate.

8.6 The plan's attempt, timed

Engine, sixteen fresh paths: an attempt lasts \(\mathbb{E}[T] = 17.54\) days on average, from the purchase to the failed phase or the closed funded account.

So one slot buys \(30.44/17.54 = 1.74\) evaluations a month and pays \(1.74\times\$607.82 = \$1{,}055\) a month in fees before refunds.

Value of an attempt: \(\mathbb{E}[V] = \$1{,}448\); pass rate 27.3%; cash per funded account $7,528.

Renewal–reward (8.3): \(30.44 \times 1{,}448 / 17.54 = \$2{,}512\) per month, ± $217 (95%, cluster-robust over 16 paths; attempt-level ± $148).

Where the time goes (paths 141–144): an attempt that fails in the evaluation lasts 10.5 days on average; one that passes spends 20.4 days in the evaluation and 14.1 days funded, with 0.54 payouts on average; 37.6 trades per attempt.

Chapter 9

Optimising the settings

Four numbers define how the plan trades a programme: the risk per trade \(L\), the reward-to-risk \(k\), the stop \(m\) and the funded payout target \(X\); a fifth choice is the market. This chapter explains what each does, how they were searched, and what the search can and cannot claim.

9.1 What each setting does

SettingValue of an attemptTime of an attemptRange searched
Risk \(L\)with the 40% cap on a win, a larger \(L\) means a smaller effective reward:riskmarket time \(\propto 1/L^2\)1.0%, 1.5%, 1.75% of the account (every firm's 2% rule stays out of reach after costs); FTMO at most 1.5%, its own guidance
Reward:risk \(k\)fewer trades, less cost, until the 40% cap bindslittle in theory (8.2)1.5 to 12
Stop \(m\)cost per trade \(\rho = \kappa/(m\sigma)\)market time \(\propto m^2\)from the leverage floor (Chapter 10) or 0.6, whichever is larger, to 1.25 (0.6: no stop or target closer than 0.6 hourly standard deviations, 4.1)
Payout target \(X\)more funded trades and cost with larger \(X\) (7.3)payout waiting against cycle length4% to 30% of the account, within each firm's payout rules (Maven: 2–4.9%, so at most $4,900)
Marketcost per unit of movement \(\kappa/\sigma\)trading hoursthose the firm's leverage and copy rules allow

9.2 The search

  1. Grid. For every programme and every market it may trade, every combination of \(L\), \(m\) (up to four stops from the leverage floor up), \(k \in \{1.5, 2, 3, 4, 6, 8, 12\}\) and five or six payout targets: 10,857 settings, each 2,000 attempts on four paths (101–104): 21.7 million simulated attempts.
  2. Refine. For each programme and market, the fourteen best grid settings, plus \(k\) half-way to its neighbours around the best (or 16 and 1.2 where the best sat at the end of the \(k\) grid) and a payout target 5 points larger, at most 30%, where the best sat at the top of its grid (not at Maven, whose grid ends at the plan's $4,900 ceiling), re-run for 16,000 attempts on four different paths (111–114), plus FTMO's best grid settings inside its 1.5% ceiling: 397 settings, 6.4 million attempts.
  3. Choose. The highest refined value per month with a stop of at least 0.6 hourly moves, a payout target of at most 30% of the account and, at FTMO, a risk of at most 1.5% (its own guidance, 10.5).
  4. Report. The chosen setting is run a third time on sixteen fresh paths (121–136), 24,000 attempts in all, with the cluster-robust interval of 8.4. Every figure reported for a programme comes from this run, so none is inflated by having been picked as the best of many noisy estimates. The inflation is visible: the chosen settings average 4% less on the fresh paths than on the paths that chose them.

What the search establishes. The best setting found in this grid, for these programmes and these markets, under the model. It is not a proof of a global optimum over every possible rule, product, size or market; Part II lists what was searched for each programme and what was left out.

9.3 What the search found

The policy moves the optimum. Under the 40% cap no win can end a phase, so the version 6 corner (k of 20–30, one trade per phase) is gone. The chosen settings use risk 1.50% (6), 1.75% (18), \(k\) from 3 to 12, stops from 0.75 to 1.25 and payout targets from 4.9% to 30% of the account.

Risk per trade. With wins capped at 40% of the target, a larger risk mainly makes the account move faster (market time \(\propto 1/L^2\), 8.2) at almost the same pass probability, which favours the largest risk the plan allows: 1.75%, set by the firms' 2% rules, and 1.5% at FTMO (10.5). 19 of 24 chosen settings use that ceiling. The others chose a smaller risk because a lower setting measured higher on the refinement paths: FundedNext Stellar on gold at 1.50% ($2,588 ± $504 in the refinement, against $2,525 ± $528 for the best setting at 1.75%); The5ers HS on Nasdaq at 1.50% ($2,737 ± $1,225 in the refinement, against $2,644 ± $962 for the best setting at 1.75%); The5ers HS Classic on Nasdaq at 1.50% ($2,971 ± $1,126 in the refinement, against $2,946 ± $655 for the best setting at 1.75%); FunderPro Classic on USDJPY at 1.50% ($2,574 ± $507 in the refinement, against $2,488 ± $496 for the best setting at 1.75%); Maven 2-Step on Nasdaq at 1.50% ($1,393 ± $97 in the refinement, against $1,356 ± $63 for the best setting at 1.75%). Where the two intervals overlap, the choice between them is within the search's noise.

Payout targets. 9 of 24 chosen settings use the plan's ceiling of 30% of the account (one payout of more than 30% invites review); a larger target means fewer payout dates to wait for and, under the 40% cap, larger wins per trade, against more funded trades (7.3). Firms with payout caps or ceilings (GFT, Maven) are held below them.

Chapter 10

Position size, leverage and margin

10.1 From risk to lots

The stop distance in price points is \(m\sigma\times\text{price}\). If one lot gains or loses \(V\) dollars per point (the firm's contract specification), then

Lots for a given risk $$\text{lots} = \frac{l}{m\,\sigma\,\text{price}\,V}, \qquad \text{notional} = \frac{l}{m\sigma}.$$

Lots are rounded down to the lot step, so the realised risk is never above \(l\). Micro Nasdaq futures trade in whole contracts of $2 per point. The synthetic Nasdaq path starts at 100, so at every entry the engine converts its price to the index level, \(P_\text{index} = 31{,}070 \times P/100\), values one contract's stop at \(2\,P_\text{index}\,m\sigma\) dollars, and rounds the number of contracts down (never below one). Version 7 used 31,070 at every entry: at a path level of 125 that understated one contract's risk by a fifth. A futures account whose room is below one contract's stop makes its terminal trade with one contract (5.4). Where one contract at the planned stop risks more than the planned \(l\) (on a path whose index has risen several-fold), the plan trades one contract if the floor and daily rooms allow it; the first version 8 engine waited instead, and those accounts never traded again (Appendix A, n11).

10.2 Leverage and margin, at every entry

A position of notional \(N\) needs margin \(N/\lambda\) at leverage 1:\(\lambda\). The plan keeps margin at most 60% of the current balance at every entry (version 6 checked it on the starting balance only):

Margin condition at entry, and the smallest stop at the start $$\frac{l}{m\sigma\lambda} \le 0.6\,(S + x) \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad l \le 0.6\,\lambda\, m\sigma\,(S+x), \qquad m_{\min} = \frac{L/S}{0.6\,\lambda\,\sigma}.$$

The engine applies the first form to every trade, lowering the risk when the balance has fallen; the search starts its stops at \(m_{\min}\). Where a firm's leverage differs between evaluation and funded accounts (GFT: 1:20 then 1:10 on indices), the funded leverage sets \(m_{\min}\). FunderPro's funded accounts have a rule of their own: at most 20% of the starting balance as margin per asset class. At 1:30 on the Nasdaq that needs \(m \ge L/(S \times 0.2 \times 30 \times \sigma)\): 1.066 at the plan's 1.75% risk (0.913 at 1.5%; version 7's rule sheet quoted 0.92, the 1.5% figure, next to a 1.75% risk). GFT's funded 1:10 with the plan's 60% cap gives the same 1.066 at 1.75%. Version 6's FunderPro setting (\(m = 0.35\)) used about 52% and broke the rule.

Leverage as published on 4 October 2026 (optimize_v8.LEV). \(m_{\min}\): the smallest stop whose margin fits 60% of the starting balance at both leverages (FunderPro funded: 20%). At every entry the engine also requires margin at most 60% of the current balance, lowering the risk if needed.

10.3 Which market each account trades

  • Nasdaq 100 (US100 CFD, or micro Nasdaq futures): every firm that allows the position and the same trades as your own accounts elsewhere.
  • USDJPY: FundingPips (leverage), and any firm whose Nasdaq stop the leverage pushes too wide, where the search found it better.
  • Markets of their own for the firms that forbid copying from other firms' accounts or into funded accounts: FundedNext and Hola Prime each trade a market (EURUSD, gold) that no other account trades.

Inside each group every account takes the same direction (Chapter 28), so no two accounts are ever on opposite sides of one market.

10.4 The ten-minute rule

FundingPips groups positions on one market and direction into one "trade idea" limited to 2% on funded accounts above $25K; Hola Prime treats a position opened within 10 minutes of closing one in the same direction and market, profitable or not, as the same trade idea, whose combined risk may not exceed 2%. The plan waits at least 10 minutes after every close before the next entry on that account, at every firm. Version 7 said that an entry at a later hour's open already met this; a close at 12:59 and an entry at 13:00 does not. The engine now knows each exit's minute from the one-minute sub-steps (3.3) and, after an exit later than minute 50, waits one more hour; tests_v8.py measures the shortest time from an exit to the next entry over thousands of consecutive trades (10382 consecutive trades: shortest time from an exit to the next entry 10 minutes).

10.5 Risk per trade

Version 6 fixed the risk at 1.5% and argued from time alone. Under the 40% cap a larger risk lowers the effective reward:risk of a capped win, so the risk now matters for value too, and it is searched (1.0%, 1.5%, 1.75%). The upper end is 1.75% because Alpha forbids risking or losing 2% or more on a trade, FundingPips closes a trade idea at 2%, Hola Prime and FunderPro cap funded risk at 2%, and Blue Guardian's shield and GFT's Goat Guard act at a 2% open loss: 1.75% plus cost stays below all of them. FTMO publishes a recommendation of 1–1.5% of the initial balance per trade and says larger risk can trigger its risk monitoring; it is not a written rule, but the plan treats it as one and caps FTMO at 1.5%.

Chapter 11

Checking the engine against the formulas

The engine and the chain were written separately. The chain leaves out daily limits, minimum and profitable days, the per-day part of the concentration policy, payout dates (it pays only at the payout target, while the plan withdraws whatever profit there is on each payout date), the daily flat, weekend closes, the 10-minute gap and Maven's 30-day window; the engine includes them. Both include the terminal rule of 5.4 and the minimum target distance of 4.1. One effect exists only in the engine and only if positions are held over a weekend: a gap through the floor is absorbed by the firm, a gap through the target is kept. The plan is flat at weekends, so it does not arise; the verification stays flat at weekends too. Agreement where both apply is supporting evidence that each implements its model as described. It is not proof that either is right: two programs can agree because they share a wrong rule input or a wrong assumption. Where they differ, the difference should come from what the chain leaves out, and the third column of 11.2 measures exactly that, by running the engine with those rules switched off.

Sub-step convergence. At the tightest stop the plan allows (0.6 hourly standard deviations), cost-free brackets on six independent two-year paths per cell (120,000 each) win at rates that differ from \(1/(1+k)\) by k = 1: -0.09 ± 0.28 points at 12, -0.08 ± 0.35 points at 60, +0.04 ± 0.28 points at 240; k = 2: -0.02 ± 0.87 points at 12, +0.01 ± 0.52 points at 60, -0.09 ± 0.40 points at 240; k = 3: -0.34 ± 0.51 points at 12, -0.06 ± 0.79 points at 60, -0.16 ± 0.55 points at 240; k = 6: -0.01 ± 0.27 points at 12, -0.24 ± 0.66 points at 60, -0.03 ± 0.56 points at 240 sub-steps per hour (the larger of the binomial and the cluster interval). Every cell lies inside its interval. These intervals say how far each estimate can be from the truth under the model; they are not a bound on the generator's error, which is the bound of 3.3 below. Version 7's claim of a quarter-point bound did not follow from its own cells and is withdrawn.

The scaling test, twice. Rules: with the 200K fee set to twice the 100K fee, a 200K attempt equals twice the matching 100K attempt, path by path, for FTMO, The5ers, Fintokei (largest difference $0.00 over 4,500 pairs). GFT is not exact, because its $3,000 day cap and $10,000 payout cap are flat amounts: $1,394 per 100K at 200K against $1,515 at 100K. Contracts: with the firms' real 200K fees, FTMO $1,845 per 100K at 200K against $1,845 at 100K (fee $1,215.65 against 2 × $607.82); Fintokei $1,454 per 100K at 200K against $1,519 at 100K (fee $1,249.00 against 2 × $549.00); GFT $1,424 per 100K at 200K against $1,515 at 100K (fee $974.00 against 2 × $524.00) (The5ers sells no 200K account of this programme, so its 200K row checks the rules only.) The fee difference is the whole of the gap where the rules scale; 27.2 values it.

The direction rule. Per trade (cost-free 1 : 3 brackets on the Nasdaq, 60,000 each), the plan's momentum rule averages +0.0040 ± 0.0143 R and a coin flip +0.0099 ± 0.0143 R. Per month (FTMO, 48,000 attempts each on paths 141–144) the momentum rule gives $2,330 ± $2,254 and the coin flip $2,330 ± $448. The momentum rule's interval is wider because attempts that overlap on one path take the same direction at the same time, so they are correlated. Under the model no rule that uses only past prices can have an edge (2.3); the plan uses the momentum rule only so that all of one person's accounts take the same side.
